# Humanizer: complete second-pass comparisons

**Reverted on 12 October 2026 at Robin’s request.** This is an archival record of the second Humanizer pass. The source readings and application copies have returned to the saved versions from before this pass, matching the “Before” examples below.

Every replacement below is exact. “Before” is the text at the start of this substantive pass, after the earlier light trim. “After” is the final canonical reading. The saved initial baseline also retains the wording before either pass.

All 132 readings were reviewed; 125 were edited and seven retained. The 305 entries below are paragraph or whole-field replacements.

## Not Only the Way · The Craftsman (caregiver-creator)

Decision: edited. Turned the repeated correction into one spoken sentence while preserving concealed pride, the quoted misunderstanding and the care motive. Joined the clipped recognition to its actual observation; the historical interpretation remains under I like to think and still leaves room for other methods. The remaining passages were retained because their scenes, affectionate turns and factual wording already carry the intended voice.

Meaning retained: motive: Care is expressed through doing a thing properly, while allowing other people's own ways.; fear: Pride can be mistaken for making the gift about oneself or claiming superiority.; gift: The careful work looks after its recipient, rather than setting a standard they must meet.; story: Uyeda's translator, quotations and test qualifications remain; the imagined wider care stays under I like to think.; drink: The unseen caraway cordial preserves the drink's appearance while adding the author's personal care.; proposal: Accept thanks and say what the work involved, rather than hiding it.

[Final source](</Users/robin.molinas/Documents/GenAI Projects/Dionysus/Cocktail_Website_Agent/design-artifacts/pours/caregiver-creator.md>)

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

What you rarely say out loud is how proud you are of it. Pride feels like making it about you, so you call it "just doing it properly" and give the credit to the thing. And once or twice, explaining your way, you've watched someone hear "my way is better". You never meant that. You meant: this is the way I know how to look after you.

**After**

What you rarely say out loud is how proud you are of it. Pride feels like making it about you, so you call it "just doing it properly" and give the credit to the thing. Once or twice, you've explained your way and watched someone hear "my way is better", when all you meant was: this is the way I know how to look after you.

Edit: Turned the repeated correction into one spoken sentence while preserving concealed pride, the quoted misunderstanding and the care motive.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. I like to think the care was never only in the drink. It was in holding himself to his way for whoever sat down in front of him, and leaving room for everyone else's. That's you. Proud of how you do it, and glad when someone else does it their own way.

**After**

3. I like to think the care was never only in the drink. It was in holding himself to his way for whoever sat down in front of him, while leaving room for everyone else's. You take pride in how you do it, and you're glad when someone else does it their own way.

Edit: Joined the clipped recognition to its actual observation; the historical interpretation remains under I like to think and still leaves room for other methods.

### yours[2]

**Before**

2. He didn't take it to a shop. He took it to the Navy. The doctors they sent him to approved it, and a trial was ordered on the King's ships. He published a way anyone could copy, with chalk and acid, and never went into the business himself. And it was no cure. His water was only water and gas.

**After**

2. He took it to the Navy rather than a shop. The doctors they sent him to approved it, and a trial was ordered on the King's ships. He published a way anyone could copy, with chalk and acid, but never went into the business himself. It was no cure: his water was only water and gas.

Edit: Gave the route to the Navy and the failed cure a natural sentence rhythm without changing the approval, trial, published method or decision not to trade.

### yours[4]

**Before**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is the plainest but crispest drink those bubbles made possible: whisky and soda, tall, over ice. Whisky starts life as a kind of beer, so I steep it with dried hops, the flower that gives beer its bitterness. That's my own nod to the vat he started with; the record doesn't say what was in his. Then very cold soda, poured slowly and stirred once, and a long strip of lemon peel, twisted once as it goes in, and left in the glass. More than two thirds of the glass is the kind of fizzy water he hoped would be a cure. It's also what makes it a highball.

**After**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is the plainest but crispest drink those bubbles made possible: whisky and soda, tall, over ice. Whisky starts life as a kind of beer, so I steep it with dried hops, the flower that gives beer its bitterness. That's my own nod to the vat he started with; the record doesn't say what was in his. Then I pour in very cold soda slowly, stir once, and add a long strip of lemon peel, twisting it once as it goes in and leaving it in the glass. More than two thirds of the glass is the kind of fizzy water he hoped would be a cure. It's also what makes it a highball.

Edit: Recast the suspended ingredient-and-method fragment as an action sentence, preserving cold soda, slow pour, single stir, single twist, retained peel and the vat qualification.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

Nobody sees the part that comes later. When everyone's safe and home, your hands start to shake, or you lie awake until four. And the quiet weeks can be harder than the bad nights, because with nobody to go out for, you're not sure what you're for. Standing back has always felt to you like the worst kind of selfishness. **After**

When everyone's safe and home, your hands start to shake, or you lie awake until four. Nobody sees that part. The quiet weeks can be harder than the bad nights: with nobody to go out for, you're not sure what you're for. Standing back has always felt like the worst kind of selfishness, so you've never let anyone come out for you. You'd rather be the one who comes.

### yours[5]

**After**

5. You won't ask for help in a crisis. I know that, and I'm not going to argue with you about it. On some ordinary evening, when nothing's wrong and nobody needs you, let someone make you this, and sit down while they do. Let them do the going, just once.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

Hardly anyone has seen you in a mood. Cross over nothing. Wanting the window seat, the last slice, an evening that's only yours. You keep all of that on the other side of the door. Somewhere you learned that being good is what keeps your place, and that wanting too much might cost you it. So the people who love you know half of you. It's the easy half, for them.

**After**

Hardly anyone has seen you in a mood, cross over nothing, or wanting the window seat, the last slice, an evening that's only yours. You keep all of that on the other side of the door. Somewhere you learned that being good keeps your place, and that wanting too much might cost you it. So the people who love you only know the half that's easy for them.

Edit: Connected the row of fragments into a recognisable sentence and made the final easy-half reference clear, preserving all three wishes and the conditional fear of losing a place.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. I think your calm works the same way. It's real, and it's generous, and it's also the safest place in the room to stand. If you're the one steadying everyone, nobody's looking at you, and nobody's cross with you, because you're never cross first. And as long as you only ever bring the calm, you never have to find out whether they'd love you with the rest. That's what it costs. Not that nobody sees the rest of you. That you never get to hear the answer.

**After**

3. I think your calm works the same way. It's real and generous, and it gives you the safest place in the room to stand. While you're steadying everyone, nobody's looking at you or getting cross with you, because you're never cross first. Keeping it to the calm spares you from finding out whether they'd love the rest of you too, and leaves you without an answer.

Edit: Kept calm as a real gift and a hiding place, while removing three sentences that repeat the already stated cost of never hearing whether the rest would be loved.

## Worth the Trip · The Reassurer (caregiver-jester)

Decision: edited. Rebuilt the stop-start timing paragraph around its actual sequence, retaining waiting, occasional withholding, relief, embarrassed laughter and the breath afterwards. Replaced the repeated inverted explanation with direct motive and fear, keeping the reassurance that the joke communicated care rather than self-protection. Meaning retained: motive: Read a hurt person and wait for the joke that lets them breathe.; fear: The guest worries the joke only made standing there easier for them.; gift: A carefully timed laugh communicates that the hurt has been seen and the person is not alone.; story: Jeff Berry's account, October 1942, four gallons, and the maybe/almost-certainly battle qualifications remain.; drink: The drink delivers the care through its cooking and warm ginger/cumin, retaining the protected paragraph.; proposal: Stay for the quiet after the laugh without reaching for another line.

[Final source](</Users/robin.molinas/Documents/GenAI Projects/Dionysus/Cocktail_Website_Agent/design-artifacts/pours/caregiver-jester.md>)

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

When something goes badly for someone, you're the one who says the thing that makes them laugh. Not straight away. You wait. You read them first, you work out how bad it is, and sometimes you keep the line to yourself because it's too early. Then, at the right moment, you say it, and for a second everything weighs a little less. They laugh, they're half embarrassed to be laughing, and then they breathe out.

**After**

When something goes badly for someone, you wait until you've read them, worked out how bad it is, and found the right moment to say the thing that will make them laugh. Sometimes you keep it to yourself because it's too early. When you do say it, everything weighs a little less for a second. They laugh, half embarrassed to be laughing, and then breathe out.

Edit: Rebuilt the stop-start timing paragraph around its actual sequence, retaining waiting, occasional withholding, relief, embarrassed laughter and the breath afterwards.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

People sometimes take it to mean you don't take things seriously. It's the other way round. You don't joke because it isn't serious. You joke because it is. The worry underneath it all is that the joke was really for you, to make it easier to stand there. It wasn't. It was how you told them you'd seen exactly how bad it was, and that they weren't in it alone.

**After**

People sometimes mistake your jokes for not taking things seriously. You joke because it is serious. Underneath, you worry the joke was really for you, to make it easier to stand there. But it was how you told them you'd seen exactly how bad it was, and that they weren't in it alone.

Edit: Replaced the repeated inverted explanation with direct motive and fear, keeping the reassurance that the joke communicated care rather than self-protection.

### yours[5]

**Before**

5. The laugh is how you get in. Don't let it become how you get out. When the laugh comes, don't reach for the second line. Let the quiet after it happen. You're good company there too, without saying a thing.

**After**

5. When you've made someone laugh, stay for the quiet afterwards instead of reaching for a second line. The laugh gets you in; it needn't get you out again. You're good company there too, without saying a thing.

Edit: Put the future action first and joined the entry/exit image to it, retaining silence, restraint and the affectionate assurance of company.

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

Someone you love walks in, or picks up the phone, and you can hear it in the first word: a bad day. You don't ask yet. You know it isn't about you, you've a fair idea what it is about, and you know they'll get there in their own time. So you stay close, and you wait. You've seen them at their worst more than once. It never changed your mind about them. It completed the picture.

**After**

When someone you love walks in, or picks up the phone, you hear a bad day in the first word. You don't ask yet. You know it isn't about you and have a fair idea what it is about, but they'll get there in their own time. So you stay close and wait. You've seen them at their worst more than once. It never changed your mind about them. It completed the picture.

Edit: Untangled the repeated you-know clauses and the opener without losing the first-word recognition, unhurried presence or the stronger picture gained through difficult days.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. That's the part of her I think you'll know. Not the telling-off: the way she did it. No scene, no leaving the room. She understood him, she stood by him, and once, she didn't let understanding stand in for saying it. I like to think she could only do it because she was never going anywhere. People who stay can afford to be honest.

**After**

3. I think you'll recognise how she did it, without a scene or leaving the room. She understood him and stood by him, but once, she didn't let understanding stand in for saying it. I like to think she could only do that because she was never going anywhere. People who stay can afford to be honest.

Edit: Turned the clipped not-the-telling-off explanation into connected prose; preserved understanding, staying, the singular challenge and its signposted motive.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

You know that help can taste bitter. Being looked after can feel like being told you're not coping, so you make yours easy to take. But the few who didn't get better stay with you. You go back over them, wondering what you missed, as if their mending were yours to finish. That's the price of believing in people before they do, and you've never once thought it wasn't worth paying.

**After**

Being looked after can feel like being told you're not coping, and you know how bitter that can taste. So you make your help easy to take. The few who didn't get better stay with you, though. You go back over them, wondering what you missed, as if their mending were yours to finish. Believing in people before they believe in themselves has cost you that, and you've never thought it wasn't worth it.

Edit: Grounded the bitter-help image in its concrete emotional cause and replaced the price aphorism with the actual cost, retaining belief, regret and its accepted value.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. I like to think he never forgot being the one handed the glass nobody wanted to drink, and that's why he didn't take the bitter out, only put something sweet beside it, so people would come back for it on their own. I can't tell you whether it made anyone better. I can tell you people kept choosing it: by 1909 it took about three million oranges a year. That's how your help works too. It stays honest, and it still gets taken.

**After**

3. I like to think he never forgot being handed a glass nobody wanted to drink, and kept the bitter with something sweet beside it so people would come back on their own. I can't tell you whether it made anyone better. People did keep choosing it: by 1909 it took about three million oranges a year. That's how your help works too: it stays honest, and people still take it.

Edit: Smoothed the long inference and the repeated I-can/I-cant setup, retaining the remembered experience, voluntary return, health uncertainty and dated consumption claim.

### yours[4]

**Before**

4. So I crafted your cocktail from his idea, not his bottle, and made it a long drink, the way people came to drink his amer in the Basque communities of the American West: with soda, a little grenadine and brandy on top. The amer in your cocktail is a modern one, made with the old Amer Picon in mind, and the rest of the glass is built around it. The grenadine is my own pomegranate syrup, with a sweet orange's peel rubbed into the sugar, so a bitter orange and a sweet one sit side by side, the way they did in 1840. Then soda, and the cognac poured last over a spoon so it rests on top. The first sip is the gentle one. The bitter comes right after, and nothing covers it.

**After**

4. I crafted your cocktail from his idea, using a modern bottle made with the old Amer Picon in mind. I've kept it long, following the way people came to drink his amer in the Basque communities of the American West: with soda, a little grenadine and brandy on top. The rest of your cocktail is built around that amer. The grenadine is my own pomegranate syrup, with a sweet orange's peel rubbed into the sugar, so a bitter orange and a sweet one sit side by side, the way they did in 1840. I add the soda, then pour the cognac last over a spoon so it rests on top. The first sip is gentle; the bitter comes right after, uncovered.

Edit: Rebuilt the awkward cocktail introduction and suspended pour instructions as full speech, preserving modern-versus-historical bottle, Basque serve, every ingredient/order and the gentle-then-bitter sensory sequence.

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

When something is unfair to someone who isn't in the room, yours is the voice that goes first. At the meeting, at the school gate, at the family table. People think it comes easily to you. It doesn't. Your heart is going before you open your mouth, you rehearsed it on the way there, and afterwards, when somebody says they wish they could do that, your hands are still shaking.

**After**

When something is unfair to someone who isn't in the room, you're the first to speak, at a meeting, the school gate or the family table. People think it comes easily to you, but you've rehearsed it on the way and your heart is going before you open your mouth. Afterwards, when somebody says they wish they could do that, your hands are still shaking.

Edit: Wove the locations and anxious preparation into sentences rather than staged fragments; kept the absent-person motive, mistaken ease and shaking afterwards.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. I like to think her heart was going when she wrote that first page, and that she wrote the rest of it all the same. You'd know the feeling. And I think you'd know the other part too: she never saw the end. Your wins mostly happen after you've moved on, in rooms you're no longer in.

**After**

3. I like to think her heart was going when she wrote that first page, and that she wrote the rest of it all the same. You'd know that feeling, and I think you'd recognise the other part of her story too: she never saw the end. Most of your wins happen after you've moved on, in rooms you're no longer in.

Edit: Removed duplicated recognition wording while retaining the signposted fear, writing anyway, historical unfinished end and the guests wins occurring elsewhere.

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

It's late, everyone's having a good time, and you're the one who says "one more, then home." You're the one who checks what's left in the account before the trip gets booked, who asks for a text when they're home, who keeps the Sunday call going long after the others have let it slide. Some people hear a rule. It isn't one, not really. It's what keeps the good thing there tomorrow.

**After**

It's late and everyone's having a good time when you say "one more, then home." ### yours[3]

3. So this glass is mine, not his. Absinthe is made with wormwood, anise and fennel, and a book of flavour pairings gives fennel an unlikely match: cranberry. The cranberry syrup goes in first, the way absinthe was sweetened before the spoon and the sugar cube came along. The absinthe goes over it, then iced water, a few drops at a time. As the water falls, the clear green turns to a pale, shifting cloud. The French call it the louche, and the same word means shady, disreputable. Its bad name and its beauty share one word. A good cloud is one of the ways you tell it was made with care. Then one stir, and the red comes up through the white and turns it rose. The feeling doesn't spoil the careful part. It's what gives it colour.

As the water falls, the clear green turns to a pale, shifting cloud. The French call it the louche, a word that also means shady or disreputable, so its bad name and its beauty share one word. A good cloud is one of the ways you tell it was made with care. One stir brings the red up through the white and turns it rose. The feeling doesn't spoil the careful part; it's what gives it colour.

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

Show you almost anything and you see what it could be: the chair someone put out on the pavement, a friend's flat, the haircut they've had since school. Once you start on it, you'll change nearly everything, except the one thing that makes it theirs. You find that part first, and build the rest around it. So the words you're hoping for at the end aren't "I'd never have recognised it." They're "That's so you."

**After**

Show you almost anything and you see what it could be, whether it's a chair someone put out on the pavement, a friend's flat or the haircut they've had since school. You find the one part that makes it theirs first, then change nearly everything around it. So the words you're hoping for at the end aren't "I'd never have recognised it." They're "That's so you."

Edit: Joined the examples to the vision and combined the repeated identity-preserving steps, while keeping both exact recognition quotes and the near-total change.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

What nobody sees is the middle. There's always a point where it looks worse than when you started: the room stripped to the walls, the paint half on, a friend at the mirror not sure what you've done. You're the only one who isn't worried. You've been through enough middles to know the mess is part of how it works. What worries you comes later, once you've gone: that it all slides back, or that it turns out in some way you didn't mean. What stays is how you looked at them: as if the best of them was worth moving everything else for. Most people have never been looked at like that.

**After**

There's always a point in the middle when things look worse than at the start: the room stripped to the walls, the paint half on, a friend at the mirror not sure what you've done. Nobody sees that part. You've been through enough middles to know the mess is part of how it works, so you're the only one who isn't worried. They catch the harsh, drying edge of the tea and carry it off with them, and what comes through is smooth. The tea flavour, he wrote, was still very strong. It went on his bar's menu. ### yours[4]

**Before**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is an Old-Fashioned, the oldest shape a cocktail has and one of the best loved, made with one single-barrel bourbon and no other whiskey. I'd pour Blanton's, or any single-barrel bourbon. The sugar is my change. Every bourbon barrel is charred inside, and the charring turns some of the wood's own sugars to caramel. So instead of the usual sugar, I do the same to it: it's cooked in a pan to deep amber, taken off the heat before it can turn bitter, and loosened with water, to bring out what the barrel already has, not to cover it. I did that for you, because you know exactly how far to go, and when to stop. Two dashes of Angostura, a long stir over ice, and it's poured, small and cold, into a tumbler from the freezer, with no ice. Last, the oil from an orange peel, for its scent, and the peel set aside.

**After**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is an Old-Fashioned, the oldest shape a cocktail has and one of the best loved, made with one single-barrel bourbon and no other whiskey. I'd pour Blanton's, or any single-barrel bourbon. I've changed the sugar to echo what happens in the barrel. Every bourbon barrel is charred inside, turning some of the wood's own sugars to caramel. I cook the drink's sugar in a pan to deep amber, take it off the heat before it can turn bitter, and loosen it with water. It brings out what the barrel already has without covering it. I did that for you, because you know exactly how far to go, and when to stop. After two dashes of Angostura and a long stir over ice, I pour it small and cold into a tumbler from the freezer, with no ice. Last comes the oil from an orange peel for its scent; the peel is set aside.

Edit: Give the caramel mechanism and preparation a natural sequence while keeping the barrel connection, quantities, serving temperature and personal nod.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

You were never dawdling. You wanted to see what it would do. You don't often say how much a small thing can make your day, because it can sound naive, and there's an old, faint guilt about holding everyone up. It isn't being slow that scares you. It's getting busy, and sure, and one day walking straight past something without feeling the tug, and not even missing it. The people who walk with you see more than they would on their own. The ordinary comes back to them new. That's what your slowness gives them, and every so often the thing you stopped for turns out to matter to everyone.

**After**

You wanted to see what it would do. You don't often say how much a small thing can make your day, because it can sound naive, and there's an old, faint guilt about holding everyone up. Your fear is that one day you'll be busy and sure of yourself enough to walk straight past something without feeling the tug, and not even miss it. The people who walk with you see more than they would on their own; the ordinary comes back to them new. That's what your slowness gives them, and every so often the thing you stopped for turns out to matter to everyone.

Edit: Join the fear and the gift into a conversational paragraph, retaining the hidden guilt and the possibility that a small discovery matters more widely.

It was the idea, and the question under it: what happens if you don't hurry? You hand people that question all the time, and some of them take it further than you'd ever have guessed.

**After**

3. I like to think Conigliaro gave that bottle its years because he trusted there was something in it, while nobody else was interested yet. ### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

Some of it falls over. You laugh first, and loudest. What you don't laugh at is the kind voice that says "you could just buy one". It's said with a smile, as common sense, and it lands harder than any collapse, because it turns the best part of your week into a waste of time. What you'd hate most is a life with nothing left to build. Here's what your long way gives people: the kitchen gets louder when you start, and an ordinary job becomes the most interesting thing in the house. When it works, nobody is more surprised than you, and the surprise is catching.

**After**

Some of it falls over, and you laugh first and loudest. But the kind voice saying "you could just buy one" lands harder than any collapse. It turns the best part of your week into a waste of time, all with a smile and the air of common sense. What you'd hate most is a life with nothing left to build. Your long way makes the kitchen louder and an ordinary job the most interesting thing in the house. When it works, nobody is more surprised than you, and the surprise is catching.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. The pot was never really an option, I like to think. You'd know. There's always a pot: the easy way, right there, that would do the job well enough. And there's the other way, the one that takes trial after trial, and leaves everybody perking up. You take the second one because it's the fun one. What makes me smile is that he was right about the iron. It does something a pot doesn't: it caramelises some of the sugar and toasts the grain, right inside the drink. The long way wasn't only more fun. To his taste, it was better. Not every time. But more often than the people who say "you could just" would like to admit.

**After**

It isn't better every time, but more often than the people who say "you could just" would like to admit.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

What you play down is how hard it hits. You say "mm, lovely", because you've seen the eyebrow go up across the table: it's only bread. It's never only bread to you. What frightens you is a life with the senses turned down, on a menu somebody else chose. Or a meal with someone who isn't tasting, which is lonelier than eating alone. And look what you do with good things. You tear off the warm end of the loaf and hand it over. You crush a leaf under someone's nose: "smell this". ### yours[4]

**Before**

The cocktail I've made for you is a Bramble, now a modern classic: gin, lemon juice and a little sugar, shaken and poured over crushed ice, then the blackberry liqueur poured slowly over the top, so it runs down through the ice in dark threads, and a fresh blackberry to finish. My change is in that pour. Into the blackberry liqueur I stir balsamic vinegar, no more than half a teaspoon, the dark, sweet Italian kind: a cook's pairing for berries. ### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

Truth is, you like the guidebook. Around people who brag about never booking anything, you leave the return ticket out. Softer still: the best night of a trip is sometimes the one after it, at your own table, telling it. Not because you didn't love being there. Because a find isn't finished until someone you love has heard about it. So the one trip you'd never book is the one with no way back: stuck somewhere, even somewhere beautiful, with no one to tell. You bring it home instead, and everyone who stayed gets to go a little. That's what you give them: the world, handed round, with directions.

**After**

You like the guidebook, though around people who brag about never booking anything, you leave the return ticket out. Harder to admit is that the best night of a trip is sometimes the one after it, at your own table, telling it. You loved being there, but a find isn't finished until someone you love has heard about it. The trip you'd never book is one with no way back: stuck somewhere, even somewhere beautiful, with no one to tell. You bring it home so everyone who stayed gets to go a little. That's what you give them: the world, handed round, with directions.

Edit: Make the private attachment to home and sharing sound spoken, keeping the guidebook embarrassment, fear of no return and distinctive gift.

### yours[3]

**Before**

3. He quotes a Mexican saying: you don't find mezcal, mezcal finds you. The villages had it all along. What he did is what you do with every good find: he didn't keep it, and he didn't pass it off as his. He brought it home and put the village's name on the bottle, and anyone who loved it knew where to send their thanks. And I like to think what hurt at the border wasn't the mezcal going down the drain. It was the friends at home who'd never taste it. You'd feel it the same way.

**After**

3. He quotes a Mexican saying: you don't find mezcal, mezcal finds you. The villages had it all along. He did what you do with every good find: brought it home to share, with the village's name on the bottle so anyone who loved it knew where to send their thanks. I like to think what hurt at the border was the thought of friends at home who'd never taste it. You'd feel it the same way.

Edit: Connect generous sharing to proper credit without repeating two negatives; keep the saying and the bartender's inference about the loss at the border.

### yours[4]

**Before**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is a Paloma, the long, bright drink people say Mexico orders instead of the Margarita, made here with mezcal instead of tequila, so it's smoky as well as sharp. I'd pour Del Maguey's Chichicapa: the village on that jug he had to pour away, and one of the first two he named on a bottle. Fresh grapefruit and a little lime give it its edge, and it's built in a tall glass, then filled with ice and lengthened with sparkling water. The sweetening is my change. Many bartenders make their own grapefruit soda, with sugar. I use honey, and I'd ask for a honey from near where you live, a jar from one place rather than a blend. Most of the drink's sweetness is that honey, so the glass doesn't work without it. That's for you. Home is half the trip for you, so it goes in with the same respect as the village. Two places, both named, and neither one the lesser. Any runny honey will do.

**After**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is a Paloma, the long, bright drink people say Mexico orders instead of the Margarita. Here it's made with mezcal instead of tequila, so it's smoky as well as sharp. I'd pour Del Maguey's Chichicapa: the village on that jug he had to pour away, and one of the first two he named on a bottle. Fresh grapefruit and a little lime give it its edge. It's built in a tall glass, then filled with ice and lengthened with sparkling water. Where many bartenders use sugar to make their own grapefruit soda, I use honey. I'd ask for a honey from near where you live, a jar from one place rather than a blend. It supplies most of the sweetness, so the glass doesn't work without it. Home is half the trip for you, and I've put it in with the same respect as the village: two places, both named, neither one the lesser. Any runny honey will do.

### whoYouAre[1]

**Before**

It was your idea in the first place: the hill, the all-day walk, the trip nobody else would have booked. ### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

You'll go as far as everyone can get back from, and not a step further. You wanted that top more than any of them. You swallow it on the spot, so nobody has to feel they cost you anything, and later, privately, you file the day under "didn't make it". The fear is getting stranded somewhere you led people, with no plan that gets every one of them home. And that's what your turning round gives the people with you: they go further in your company than they'd dare alone, because they know you'll turn round in time. You earn that trust every time you turn.

**After**

You'll go as far as everyone can get back from, and not a step further. You wanted that top more than any of them, but you swallow it on the spot so nobody has to feel they cost you anything. **After**

The lights go out in the middle of a party, and you already know where the fuse box is. The car won't start on the way to the airport, and you've got the number saved. Afterwards, you shrug: anyone would have done the same. But you know that isn't quite true. You found the fuse box the week you moved in. You drove the route the day before. You don't trust yourself to be at your best when it happens, so you prepare while everything's ordinary.

Edit: Keeps preparation driven by distrust of crisis performance without repeating 'the day' as a slogan.

### yours[4]

**Before**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is built like that pitcher, for six: a Daiquiri in the shape the family story gives it. White rum and fresh lime, with sugar stirred into the lime until it's gone, swizzled on crushed ice for thirty seconds and poured straight away into six small chilled tumblers, so nobody waits longer than anyone else. A Daiquiri always asks for some quick thinking, because limes vary in sourness from one fruit to the next. I've measured everything a measure can fix, and the limes are yours to judge. My only addition is four makrut lime leaves, pressed into the lime and lifted out before you pour. Makrut is a different lime, with harsh juice but fragrant leaves, so the answer comes from the part of the tree you'd never think to squeeze. The leaves bring lime's scent without making the drink any sharper. They're there as one more answer to his worry, and as a nod to the question you always ask first: what else is there? It's icy, bright and fresh, and the lime carries right through it.

Grappa is a brandy distilled from the grape skins, stems and seeds left once the juice is pressed out for wine. I shake it until the shaker's uncomfortable to hold, then pour it off the ice into a coupe that's been waiting in the freezer. I've taken a little of the lemon out so it's bright rather than sharp, and added five drops of salted water. The water isn't meant to taste salty. **Before**

You're watching every face. You'd never explain one of your jokes, because explaining feels like taking it apart while everyone watches. For a second nobody has to know how to feel, and the night everyone's still describing weeks later is usually one you were at.

**After**

You're watching every face. You never explain a joke; it feels like taking it apart while everyone watches. When one doesn't land, you keep a straight face and take it home with you. ### yours[4]

**Before**

4. The cocktail I've made for you starts from Croswell's four things, with a spirit of my choosing: aged rum from Jamaica, a little bitters, and a measure of water, as his definition has it, poured in ice-cold, all served without ice in a glass frozen beforehand. Last comes a bright pinch of orange oil, with its peel dropped in. My one change is the sugar: it arrives as a banana liqueur, a liqueur whose recipes only turn up about a century later. Many aged Jamaican rums already have a taste of stewed banana in them. I put it in plain sight as a nod to Croswell's line: the banana that's often already in the rum, said out loud. Expect something rich, round and smooth, and far better than it sounds when you say "banana" at a dinner party.

**After**

4. The cocktail I've made for you starts from Croswell's four things. I chose aged rum from Jamaica for the spirit, with a little bitters and a measure of water, as his definition has it. The water goes in ice-cold, and I serve it without ice in a glass frozen beforehand. Last comes a bright pinch of orange oil, with its peel dropped in. My one change is the sugar, which arrives as a banana liqueur; recipes for that liqueur only turn up about a century later. Many aged Jamaican rums already taste of stewed banana. I put it in plain sight for you, as a nod to Croswell's line: the banana that's often already in the rum, said out loud. Expect something rich, round and smooth, and far better than it sounds when you say "banana" at a dinner party.

Edit: Unwinds the long recipe sentence and duplicate liqueur wording while keeping the four-part definition and the conspicuous-banana joke.

### whoYouAre[2]

**Before**

You'll do a thing forty ways without complaint, because every one of them can still be undone. It's the last step you put off: the moment it's served, sent, signed or said, and can't be taken back. So it waits, sometimes long after it's ready. What you hide even better is the hope. People see someone careful, a little sceptical, who wants to see proof. Underneath, you believe almost anything can become something better, and you want that to be true more than you'd ever say before it works. Give you something everyone else has written off, and in time you'll hand back a better one. The people close to you start to believe things can get better, because they've seen you make it so.

**After**

You'll do a thing forty ways without complaint, because every one can still be undone. What you put off is serving it, sending it, signing it or saying it, when you can't take it back. Sometimes it waits long after it's ready. People see someone careful and a little sceptical, wanting to see proof. You believe almost anything can become better, though you won't say how much you want it to be true until it works. Give you something everyone else has written off, and in time you'll hand back a better one. Seeing you do that lets the people close to you believe things can get better too.

Edit: Untangle the final-step fear and hidden hope, and connect the concrete improvement to the confidence it gives others without three repetitions of the same better-things explanation.

**Before**

People think you do it for the gasp. The gasp is lovely, but what you really practise for is the second after, when two people who've never met look at each other and laugh. You're the only one there who knows how it's done, so you don't get the wonder. You get their faces, and that's what you came for. What you'd rarely say is how much it matters, and how much you dread the silence where that laugh should be, in front of people who owe you nothing. That's what the hours are against, and what they buy is a minute in which you turn a bus stop full of strangers into a room. Very few people can do that with a pack of cards.

**After**

People think you do it for the gasp, and the gasp is lovely. But you practise for the second after, when two people who've never met look at each other and laugh. You know how it's done, so you don't get their wonder; you get their faces, which is what you came for. You rarely say how much that matters, or how you dread the silence where the laugh should be, in front of people who owe you nothing. Those hours of practice help you turn a bus stop full of strangers into a room for a minute. That's what you give them with a pack of cards.

### whoYouAre

**Before**

Most people pick up a knack from living among strangers: looking through someone. The person sitting by the cash machine with a paper cup, the one talking to nobody on the bus, the tourist turning the map round for the third time. Everyone else's eyes slide past. Your eyes stop. You say hello. You ask where they're trying to get to. For a minute, someone everyone had stopped seeing is just a person in the queue again.

What people miss is the cost. The ones you walk past, because you were late or tired, follow you home for longer than all the times you stopped. You'd hate to be called good. You're just more afraid of a street where everyone has stopped looking, where anyone could end up being stepped round, you included. The people you stop for get to be someone, not a problem. And everyone watching learns that stopping is normal.

**After**

Living among strangers, most people get good at looking through them: the person by the cash machine with a paper cup, the one talking to nobody on the bus, the tourist turning the map round for the third time. Your eyes stop where everyone else's slide past. You say hello or ask where they're trying to get to, and for a minute, someone everyone had stopped seeing is just a person in the queue again.

The ones you walk past follow you home, even when you were late or tired. They stay with you longer than all the times you stopped. You'd hate to be called good; what frightens you is a street where everyone has stopped looking and anyone could end up being stepped round, you included. The people you stop for get to be someone, not a problem, and everyone watching learns that stopping is normal.

Someone says it in the pub, on the doorstep or in the group chat: somebody really ought to do something about it. The broken bench, the club with no treasurer, the school play nobody will run. Nobody hears about the evenings: the videos, the forums, the cousin who might know. You're out of your depth most of the way through, and you know it. A hair grip, a folded bit of card, tape from the kitchen drawer: you use whatever's there, and it works. It looks a bit odd. It lasts for years.

At a party where nothing's broken, you're not quite sure where to stand, and a dripping tap in the kitchen feels like being let in. What gets to you is the shrug, and "just get a new one", because a world that throws things away has less room for you in it. **After**

You'd rather not admit that you like knowing. It's a kind of power, though you wouldn't use the word. And when someone offers you a seat where things get decided, you come up with a reason to say no. **After**

**Before**

Every summer there's the same street party, and every summer a few more people find a reason to miss it. You're the one who suggests the Saturday instead of the Sunday, and the neighbours nobody ever thought to knock for. You keep the food everyone brings, the old songs, the raffle with the terrible prizes. Someone who's been coming for thirty years tells you it won't be the same. It isn't. The next summer, the street is full.

Nobody guesses that you love the old version more than the people defending it. You argue with dates and numbers, because "I'd hate to lose this" sounds soft, so the ones who love it too can hear someone wrecking it. The row doesn't scare you. What does is the change you pushed for turning out to be what finishes it off, and everyone remembering it was you. And look what you leave instead: the things people love are still there, with more people in them, because someone cared enough to change them. Nobody says so at the time. Someone should.

**After**

The next summer, the street is full.

You love the old version more than the people defending it, and nobody guesses. You argue with dates and numbers because "I'd hate to lose this" sounds soft. To the people who love it too, that can sound like wrecking it. You can bear the row; you dread being remembered for the change that finally finished it off. But the things people love are still there, with more people in them, because you cared enough to change them. You deserve to hear that, even when nobody says it at the time.

The last train's cancelled, and there are twelve of you on a cold platform. Someone sighs. You make a joke, it lands, and somewhere in the middle of it there's a plan, and everyone hears it: the café that's still open, the walk across town, a bet on who gets home first. Years later, it's the night everyone brings up.

Nobody calls it being in charge, because to them it feels like being asked along. You like it like that. Being obeyed bores you, and it would turn you into the boss. You seldom admit how much you enjoy having your hand on the wheel, and that when you make that first joke, you don't yet know it'll work. What you dread is the night it gets away from you, or the night the fun runs out and you're just someone telling people where to go. Around you, a plan falling apart is where the good part begins. People go further than they ever would on their own, and they come back glad they went.

**After**

On an ordinary day you can seem like too much. You take the long view when the rest of the room is busy with the week, and you mention what could go wrong before anyone else is worried. People nod, and talk about something else. Then the worst day comes, the one where everyone else is lost for words, and they all look at you. You've had the words for a while.

You don't say how much the quiet stretches sting. Seeing it coming isn't much comfort while nobody's listening. And part of you, a part you'd rather not mention, steadies in a crisis, because at last you make sense. What you lie awake over is being the one in charge on the day the storm wins, and being remembered for that. So you work out what you'd say long before anyone asks, and on the day people have nothing left to stand on, you hand them a sentence that holds. People get through their worst days because of how you talk them through it.

**After**

Someone rings with a decision they can't make and asks what you'd do. You could tell them. Usually you don't, at least not at first. You ask what they've noticed so far, you say what you'd keep an eye on, and you let them get to the end of the sentence themselves. It takes longer. The one thing you couldn't live with is someone following you into a wrong answer. What they work out for themselves is theirs, to keep or to change.

Then one day they stop ringing. They've got it. Years later they explain something to you, faster and better than you'd have managed, and you feel two things at once: very proud, and slightly left behind. You only ever mention the first. That's better than any answer you could have handed them: they leave able to manage on their own, and sure they always could.

**After**

You ask what they've noticed, say what you'd keep an eye on, and let them finish the sentence themselves. It takes longer, but you couldn't live with leading someone into a wrong answer. They can keep or change what they work out for themselves.

One day they stop ringing because they've got it. Years later, they explain something to you faster and better than you'd have managed. You feel proud and slightly left behind, and only mention the pride. They've left able to manage on their own, sure they always could. That's more than you could have given them by handing over an answer.

Two hours into the evening, the table is arguing over who said what, whose fault it was, what to call it. You followed that sort of argument once and found how little was worth the heat. Now you let it go, except for the one small thing buried in the noise that nobody else noticed was the point. That you hold onto.

You had to learn that ease. You've been pulled into someone else's story before and found yourself carrying part of something untrue, so now you step back early. People sometimes read that as not caring. You care about very few things, and about those all the way.

With you, nobody has to win. The temperature drops, and when people want an argument to go quiet, they bring it to you. They rarely say how much that gives them.

### whoYouAre

**Before**

You know when the boiler was last serviced, what the meter read in March, and how long bread really takes in your oven, not what the packet says. When an argument starts about how things used to be, you don't join in. You fetch the notes.

Some people mistake that for coolness. It isn't. Writing it down is how you keep a messy world still enough to think in. And, though you rarely admit it, it's one of the things you enjoy most. You're better company than your notebook suggests.

What haunts you is the thing you never thought to write down, the one that mattered after all, in a column you didn't make. But when someone needs to know what really changed, nobody has to guess. They count from your numbers, and the count holds.

**After**

You know when the boiler was last serviced, what the meter read in March, and how long bread takes in your oven, whatever the packet says. When an argument starts about how things used to be, you fetch the notes.

Writing things down keeps a messy world still enough to think in. It's one of the things you enjoy most, though you rarely admit it. Some people mistake it for coolness. You're better company than your notebook suggests.

The detail you never thought to record haunts you: the one that matters later in a column you didn't make. When people need to know what changed, though, they can count from your numbers without guessing. The count holds.

### yours 4

**Before**

4. So the cocktail I've made for you is a true classic, the whisky highball: whisky and soda, which took over from brandy and soda in Britain in the second half of the nineteenth century. It's an unpeated Highland single malt, ideally one whose label says it was aged in bourbon casks, lengthened with two parts very cold soda. It's built step by step, and every step has a reason. A frozen glass and cold soda keep the bubbles. The first cube is lowered in slowly, so it doesn't crack. The bottom cube is lifted once, to mix it without knocking out the fizz. I've added one touch: a little real vanilla, as a nod to the casks his book hardly mentions, since oak casks give whisky some of that same flavour. It's there in the recipe, like everything else.

**After**

4. The cocktail I've made for you is a true classic, the whisky highball: whisky and soda, which took over from brandy and soda in Britain in the second half of the nineteenth century. I use an unpeated Highland single malt, ideally labelled as aged in bourbon casks, and two parts very cold soda. Each step has its reason. The glass is frozen and the soda cold to keep the bubbles. Lower the first cube slowly so it doesn't crack, then lift the bottom cube once to mix without knocking out the fizz. I've added a little real vanilla for the casks his book barely mentions; oak gives whisky some of that same flavour. The vanilla is written into the recipe, like everything else.
